TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* To Understand France’s Unrest, Look Back to 1982
  Foreign Policy | 08 Oct 19:44 UTC
  François Mitterrand’s U-turn decades ago holds lessons for today.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In May 1981, I watched on with fascination from a former French colony, Ivory Coast, as France elected François Mitterrand as president, seeming to give the country’s Socialist Party a mandate to break with the constraints of liberal capitalism and remake the economy along more generous and equitable lines. Fresh out of school and fired up by idealism, I was cheered by the premise behind the Socialists’ agenda for change: that a rich Western country could afford to reject capitalist orthodoxy and boldly reimagine its social and economic compact. In its first months in office, Mitterrand’s team raised wages, lowered the retirement age, shortened the workweek, and extended vacations, among other reforms. To make this work, the government took control of banks and nationalized some of France’s leading industrial companies. In May 1981, I watched on with fascination from a former French colony, Ivory Coast, as France elected François Mitterrand as president, seeming to give the country’s Socialist Party a mandate to break with the constraints of liberal capitalism and remake the economy along more generous and equitable lines. Fresh out of school and fired up by idealism, I was cheered by the premise behind the Socialists’ agenda for change: that a rich Western country could afford to reject capitalist orthodoxy and boldly reimagine its social and economic compact. In its first months in office, Mitterrand’s team raised wages, lowered the retirement age, shortened the workweek, and extended vacations, among other reforms. To make this work, the government took control of banks and nationalized some of France’s leading industrial companies. Yet this moment of stirring can-do by the state gave way, just a year later, to an abrupt U-turn. In July 1982, Mitterrand ordered a cut in public spending, raised taxes, froze prices, and restored the finance ministry’s traditional role of controlling inflation, as opposed to loosening the money supply to fund expansive social programs. I am reaching back four decades not out of nostalgia but to provide context for the social and political drama that is sweeping France today. A proper grasp of the past is key to understanding the present, and many of the lessons from this moment in history apply not only to France but to a growing number of Western countries whose economies are increasingly wracked by crisis and their politics swept up in populism.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/08/france-protests-macron-europe-history-mitterrand-economy/

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. African countries continue to push back against Western institutions that they believe fail to meet their needs. The Africa Credit Rating Agency (AfCRA) hopes to rectify an issue about which many Africans have long complained —that the current credit rating landscape overemphasizes risk across the continent, leading to excessively expensive borrowing costs for African states and institutions. Although experts have widely differing views on the likely efficacy of the AfCRA, the AU is pushing full steam ahead with this new agency. In a move that follows other efforts to gain greater self-sufficiency on the global stage, the African Union (AU) launched its new credit rating agency (CRA) on Oct. 7. The Africa Credit Rating Agency (AfCRA) hopes to rectify an issue about which many Africans have long complained —that the current credit rating landscape overemphasizes risk across the continent, leading to excessively expensive borrowing costs for African states and institutions. Although experts have widely differing views on the likely efficacy of the AfCRA, the AU is pushing full steam ahead with this new agency. The vast majority of credit ratings are determined by the Western-based “big three” CRAs—Moody’s, Standard & Poor’s (S&P), and Fitch. Together, these companies’ ratings shape borrowing costs for more than 95 percent of the world’s rated debt, encompassing sovereign actors, multilateral institutions, and private corporations. The big three CRAs began providing credit ratings to African sovereigns in 1994 , when South Africa received the continent’s first credit rating. In 2003 , the role of CRAs in Africa expanded substantially when the United Nations Development Program (UNDP) partnered with S&P to offer credit ratings to 13 African countries. At the time, many experts believed that the introduction of the Western big three CRAs in Africa would help improve the continent’s financial credibility for major global financial players, which would help attract capital, even though there was also concern that poor ratings and weak governance would increase the continent’s debt burden. The introduction of CRAs has unquestionably improved Afr
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/08/africa-credit-rating-agency-borrowing-debt/

* The Real Danger May Be AI as Dumb as Us

If you’ve been following AI debates for a while, “superintelligence,”—albeit spelled as one word, not two—immediately brings to mind the 2014 book of that name by the Swedish philosopher Nick Bostrom, a foundational text for those who see AI as an existential risk to humanity. U.S. President Donald Trump’s rechristening of artificial intelligence as “super intelligence” seems unlikely to stick, executive order notwithstanding. But it was at least good for some ironic smirks among the very same AI “ doomers ” and effective altruists that the administration has spent the past few weeks bashing. If you’ve been following AI debates for a while, “superintelligence,”—albeit spelled as one word, not two—immediately brings to mind the 2014 book of that name by the Swedish philosopher Nick Bostrom, a foundational text for those who see AI as an existential risk to humanity. But the doomer vs. accelerationist debate—over whether super-intelligent AI, or “artificial general intelligence,” will usher in either an age of unfathomable prosperity or the literal apocalypse—sidesteps an uncomfortable reality. AI is already capable of causing a global catastrophe, not because it’s an all-powerful machine god that sees puny humans as a hindrance to its goals, or is misaligned with human values to the point that it turns us all into paperclips , or even is used to design a new bioweapon. It’s dangerous simply because people increasingly trust it even though it still screws up all the time. Some of the most concerning security risks from AI may come not from super intelligence but from what researchers sometimes call “ jagged intelligence ,” the way in which models can appear to be vastly more knowledgeable than humans in many respects but still lag bafflingly behind what we’d call common sense. When we put trust in those systems anyway, disasters can happen. AI targeting systems have likely already been involved in mistakes resulting in civilian casualties in Gaza and Iran . In September, reports emerged that a hallucinating AI had helped generate an intelligence report that reportedly nearly led to the U.S. military 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/08/ai-jagged-super-intelligence-mistakes/

In fact, Trump’s tariffs, sanctions, and war against Iran have pushed Russia and China even closer together. It is time for the United States to reassess its passive approach to the two powers that most threaten U.S. interests and values. Given the depth and breadth of the Russia-China alliance and its centrality to both Putin and Xi, Washington cannot easily drive wedges between its two main antagonists, much less pit them against each other like the reverse Nixon argument suggests. * Securitize Brings Nvidia, Apple and Amazon Onchain With Tokenized Stocks on Solana
  Decrypt | 08 Oct 15:10 UTC [also carried by: The Block, The Defiant]
  The new Securitize Stocks product launches with 12 names backed one-to-one by real shares—preserving dividends and voting rights—trading first on Solana, with NYSE and OKX-ICE venues planned.
  FULL TEXT: Tokenization platform Securitize is bringing a dozen of the biggest U.S. stocks to crypto traders, launching tokenized equities backed one-to-one by real shares, with Solana as the first network. Announced Wednesday, the new Securitize Stocks product opens with security entitlements to 12 widely held names, including Apple, Microsoft, Nvidia, Google, Tesla, Meta, Amazon, Netflix, Circle, Strategy and Palantir. The tokens will initially trade on Securitize's registered broker-dealer platform on Solana , settling in the USDC stablecoin , and will be available to eligible investors in the U.S., the European Union, and other permitted jurisdictions. What sets the offering apart, the company said, is what sits behind each token. Rather than simply tracking a stock's price, as many offshore tokenized-equity products do, each Securitize Stock is backed one-to-one by an underlying share and represents a security entitlement under the Uniform Commercial Code, preserving investor rights and economic benefits like dividends and, where applicable, voting. The shares backing the tokens won't be lent out, and holders can convert their entitlements into actual registered shares where an issuer supports it. "Tokenized stocks should give investors more than a price on a wrapper that tracks a stock and is only offered offshore," said Carlos Domingo, Securitize's chairman and CEO, framing the aim as bringing equities onchain "without leaving behind the ownership, investor protections and market infrastructure that make U.S. capital markets work." Trading infrastructure is still expanding. At launch, liquidity will run through Securitize's Solana-based automated market maker, with Jump Trading serving as market maker and trading available during extended hours before a planned move to 24/7. The release names a roster of partners, including Ripple Prime and Aave, as the asset becomes usable across onchain lending and collateral markets. The launch lands in a fast-accelerating race to put stocks on blockchain networks. Securitize's own shares have traded on Solana since its NYSE listing in July , while the SEC's " innovation exemption " opened a compliant path for tokenized stocks, and rivals from Coinbase to the NYSE and an OKX-ICE venture have moved into the space. Securitize said its tokens are expected to trade on the NYSE's planned 24/7 venue and the OKX-ICE tokenized-securities venue, though neither has launched.
  https://decrypt.co/380458/securitize-nvidia-apple-amazon-tokenized-stocks-solana

--- Editorial (36) ---

* Inside the Subreddit Where Men Share Their Dinners—And Their Most Intimate Feelings
  Longreads | 08 Oct 14:48 UTC
  "A months-old subreddit called r/BoyDinnerDiares has drawn over 4 million users and become a place for men to spill their guts. Why there and why now?"
  FULL TEXT: r/BoyDinnerDiaries is a subreddit where men post photos of their often rather sad-looking meals alongside their most private worries. Zack Hauptman carefully explores this unusual place for community and care, meeting the men who are learning to share more than just what is on their plate. We hear a lot about the “male loneliness epidemic,” so it’s heartening to glimpse even a small shift in the other direction. Others use the forum regardless of whether they have a vast social circle. Robert*, 31, says he has many people to turn to but uses Reddit when he needs to unload his feelings further. “I have an urge to vent when I don’t want to call a friend,” he says. In August, after experiencing repeated rejection from women he was seeing, he decided to post about it online. “I’m at the point where I feel so disappointed and let down but also so grateful that I have the opportunity to learn and grow from all of it,” he wrote alongside a photo of mushy canned tuna in a bowl. “I could talk about it pretty much all the time, but I don’t really want to be doing that,” he says. “Going to the subreddit…it’s like throwing it into the void.” “At the Men of War Crucible, you bear-crawl through rivers. At Warrior Week, you dig your own grave.   New Lines | 08 Oct 10:57 UTC
  In the last war, the people of Tigray rallied around the TPLF. We felt we had no choice but to remain united and fight if we were to save ourselves. This time, things will be different.   FULL TEXT: Like many Tigrayans, I live in exile from my homeland, Ethiopia’s northernmost region, bordering Eritrea. We are a people scattered by war. Since mid-August, we have been hearing about bombs falling on Tigray, attacks carried out on a near-daily basis by Ethiopian fighter jets and drones. And since Sept. 23, ground battles have been raging in the region, which is sliding into war yet again. Those who decided on war for you, my country, may they lose their eyes.” Which leaders, I ask myself, deserve to be eyeless? Because someone keeps deciding on war for Tigray, time after time. My grandfather used to tell me stories of the 1930s, when Ethiopia was invaded by Mussolini’s fascist army, and Tigray was bombed with mustard gas. My father lived through the horrors of the long war against the Derg, a brutal military dictatorship that seized power in Ethiopia in 1974. In 1991, the Derg was overthrown by the Tigray People’s Liberation Front (TPLF), a grassroots rebel movement with mass popular support. You will not find a single family in Tigray that did not lose someone during those 17 years of armed struggle. The TPLF’s victory was supposed to usher in a new era of peace and development. But in 1998 it led Ethiopia into a devastating border war against Eritrea. My uncle fought and died in that conflict, one death among tens of thousands on both sides. My turn came in 2020. That year Ethiopia’s federal government — led by an upstart prime minister named Abiy Ahmed — joined forces with Eritrea and militias from the Amhara region to wage war on Tigray. Loads of tuna and queenfish lay in rows on the tiled floor around him. Each day, clients show up at his desk, weigh fish, and make purchases or settle sales. Rahimi uses a phosphor-green highlight marker to strike the deal. Then the clients put their notebooks on his desk to have the payment confirmation stamped in them. The ritual acts make him resemble a judge. By 6 a.m. he had already sold 2 tons of fish. “My sales have dropped by 30% since last year, but thank God the business is still running well,” Rahimi said, with the typical relaxed southern Iranian accent, elongating the words’ last vowel. In his view, the rapid inflation and loss of purchasing power of the population have contributed to the drop, in addition to the maritime siege initiated by the U.S. Navy, which means fishers “cannot sail into deep waters, resulting in smaller catches and less variety. Numerous times they’ve struck vessels when they went farther.” By 7 a.m., as the humidity rose and the heat became unbearable, Rahimi locked up his chair and desk and left his mobile office at the country’s largest loading site for seafood, which is a major economic lifeline for southern Iran, with an estimated annual yield of more than 800,000 tons. The marketplace is only a stone’s throw away from the parking space of a nearby vessel that was struck twice on July 8 and 9 this year. More than 30 boats and their fishing accessories were destroyed. * The ghetto on the lagoon
  Aeon | 08 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Sixteenth-century Venice wanted to expel its Jews but couldn’t do without them. Its compromise was the world’s first ghetto - by Alexander Lee Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Ghetto Novo, Venice, November 2018. Photo by Antonio Violi/Alamy On 29 March 1516, the Venetian Senate voted to create the world’s first ‘ghetto’. All Jews then living in the city – as well as any others who might come later – were ordered to move immediately to a small island in the northeastern sestiere of Cannaregio, a neighbourhood known as the geto novo . It was anything but appealing. Previously used as a dumping ground for the communal copper foundry, it had been converted into a residential quarter for local workers. From the outside, it already looked ‘like a castle’ – ringed by canals and bounded by perilously tall tenements. But by the time Venice’s Jews were herded in, it seemed more like a prison. All outward-facing doors and windows were blocked up; extra walls were built to close off access to the water; and a pair of sturdy iron gates were placed at either end of the bridge leading to Cannaregio. These were closed at sunset and opened in the morning at the ringing of the marangona bell. And just to be sure that no one could sneak out when they weren’t supposed to, four guards – paid for by the Jews themselves – patrolled the canals around the Ghetto ‘day and night’. View of Venice ( c 1500) by Jacopo de’ Barbari, just prior to the Ghetto’s establishment; the location of the Ghetto is outlined. Courtesy Wikipedia It was not the first time that Jews had ever been confined to a separate quarter, of course. By the early 16th century, Jews in France and in German lands had already been living in designated neighbourhoods, physically distinct from the surrounding areas, for close on 300 years. Nor was it even the first time Venice itself had toyed with the idea of Jewish confinement. Back in the 13th century, it had already obliged Jews in its Cretan colony’s capital Candia (now Heraklion) to live in a separate area. What made the Ghetto unusual was its name. Etymologically speaking, of course, the word geto had nothing to do with Jews. Derived from the verb gettare , meaning ‘to throw’ or ‘cast’, it was just the local name for a foundry ( geto ) – and, by extension, for the dump next door. But even after the Venetian government moved the foundry elsewhere, people kept on calling the area by its old name. And when the Jews moved in, the island soon lent its name to the institution of segregation, too – a bit like how, today, we can use ‘the White House’ to refer both to 1600 Pennsylvania Avenue and to the administration that’s based there. This w
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-making-of-the-ghetto-on-a-venetian-island-in-1516

Equally significant were the technology’s social costs, from loss of jobs and invasions of privacy to mental health issues. Indeed, the focus on potential risks obscured AI’s actual impact on society and the environment. Paradoxically, the alarm was also increasing the prestige of AI companies: If it can destroy the world, it must have God-like powers. * Conflict in the Red Sea is forcing cloud giants to find a new route for the internet
  Rest of World | 07 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Fishermen motor past a commercial vessel anchored off Yemen's coast at Bab al-Mandeb, in the straits connecting the Red Sea with the Gulf of Aden and the Indian Ocean on September 12, 2026. Yemen's Houthis took over on September 11, 2026, a Red Sea island of Mayyun that is located in the middle of the Bab al-Mandeb shipping lane, completi...
  FULL TEXT: U.S. cloud giants are facing steep prices to secure backup internet routes around the Red Sea as fighting rages over the Red Sea strait that carries most data cables between Europe and Asia. More than 90% of data capacity between Europe and Asia runs through cables under the Red Sea. The cables enter the sea at Bab al-Mandab, a strait less than 32 kilometers (20 miles) wide between Yemen and the African nations of Djibouti and Eritrea . The Houthis, a U.S.-designated terrorist organization, seized Yemen’s side of the strait in September, including the island of Mayyun. On October 5, Saudi-backed government forces said they had retaken key positions around the strait and the port of Mocha — claims the Houthis denied . In September, Google bought a pair of fiber strands running along Turkish state pipelines, paying two to three times the roughly 6 million euros ($7 million) that newer lines crossing Turkey are expected to cost, said a person familiar with the deal, requesting anonymity because the details are not public. Long-distance fiber is usually sold with a single upfront payment covering 15 years or more of use. Buyers also pay a separate annual fee for maintenance. Microsoft said on September 23 that it plans to invest more than $400 million in subsea and land links across the Middle East by 2030. Iraq also signed a contract with Qatari telecom company Ooredoo on September 10 to carry data toward Europe through an underground route from the southern port of Al-Faw. Almost all major cloud companies are looking for backup routes around the Red Sea, the person told Rest of World . Google and Meta have started sending some live traffic through a land route across Iraq that they had previously held in reserve, the person said. Google and Meta did not respond to Rest of World ’s requests for comment by the time of publication. With Yemen’s side of the strait now contested, a single cable cut there could take months to fix, because repair crews need permission and safety guarantees to work in the water, industry experts told Rest of World . Switching to another sea cable does not help because almost all of them pass through the same narrow gap, they said. “You can have capacity on multiple cables, but if they’re all routed through the same tight expanse of water, suddenly your route resilience isn’t looking so resilient,” said Paul Brodsky, senior research manager at TeleGeography, a U.S. research firm that tracks telecommunications infrastructure worldwide
  https://restofworld.org/2026/google-meta-red-sea-subsea-cables-houthi-yemen/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* Two Films About Gaza and Why Some People Hate Them
  New Lines | 06 Oct 20:10 UTC
  The documentary film “NAZA” and the satirical drama “Yes!,” both by Israeli directors, hold up mirrors to Israeli society that most find too difficult to see. The post Two Films About Gaza and Why Some People Hate Them appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Just before the lights dimmed for a screening of “NAZA” at a Montreal cinema, someone seated to my left ripped open a cellophane bag of gummy bears and popped two into her mouth. Another woman, seated to my right, rummaged loudly in a bag of popcorn. I was reminded of an experience that Guardian journalist Jonathan Freedland describes in his gripping book “The Escape Artist: The Man Who Broke Out of Auschwitz To Warn the World.” It’s about Rudolf Vrba, a Slovakian Jew whom Claude Lanzmann interviews in “Shoah,” his epic nine-hour documentary about the systematic genocide of the Jews of Europe during World War II. When Freedland, then 19, saw “Shoah” in early 1980s London, the friend who accompanied him brought a bag of popcorn into the screening room; as he began to eat it, chewing loudly, a woman who had “an accent thick with the sound and memories of pre-war Europe” leaned over and said to him: “Have you no respect?” Lanzmann’s film makes an appearance in “NAZA,” the searing documentary by Israeli investigative journalists Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor, as do many other Holocaust references. Jonathan Glazer, whose 2023 film “Zone of Interest” depicts the lives of Auschwitz Commandant Rudolf Höss and his family in their idyllic home next to the death camp, is a “NAZA” executive producer; his influence on the production is clear. For 12 years, the prevailing understanding was that Yemen’s conflict was about central control: Whoever held Sanaa held the levers of the state, commanded the army and could shape the country’s future. As a science journalist , I’ve learnt that often the actual problem is that each study is reported in isolation, rather than weighed against everything else we know. And sometimes, the risks of harm are high. For example, in September 2025, I reported on the US Food and Drug Administration’s announcement that it would add a warning label to the painkiller acetaminophen (paracetamol), claiming that taking the drug during pregnancy increased a child’s risk of autism. It made global headlines. Yet looking closer at the science, it was clear that the FDA and its parent health department had cherrypicked a few studies, and downplayed other more robust findings. This was confirmed a couple of months later when researchers published a major review that better represented the full body of knowledge. ‘Existing evidence does not clearly link maternal paracetamol use during pregnancy with autism or ADHD in offspring,’ it concluded. By then, of course, countless women had been needlessly scared about a painkiller that is widely considered one of the safest to take during pregnancy. In 2025, academics worldwide published about 7 million scholarly articles ­– that’s more than 19,000 each day. On the surface, that might seem like a number to celebrate, but it also poses a problem. As the volume of research balloons, it can be hard to discern what these millions of papers actually tell us. Within the firehose, there are rigorous methodologies and important findings, but also baffling contradictions, unconfirmed results and sloppy science. And some diverse forms of knowledge, such as lived experience and Indigenous insights, are rarely captured in scholarly articles and databases at all. The causes are systemic. Scientists publish and promote one paper after another because that’s how they advance in their careers. Journalists breathlessly chase the latest, flashiest studies so their headlines get clicks online.   FULL TEXT: In the experimental short All-around Junior Male , the Inuk filmmaker Lindsay McIntyre documents a traditional Inuit game known as the one-foot high kick. The game was once used by hunters to indicate a successful hunt and demands extraordinary strength, balance and control. In her portrait, McIntyre captures the Nunamiut athlete Sean Uquqtuq in action with monochrome, hand-processed film that abstracts the landscape around him, focusing on his face and the movement of his body. Combined with an ambient score, these elements lend the piece an immersive, dreamlike quality. * The AI boom is making the world’s cheapest smartphones disappear
  Rest of World | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  AI data centers are driving demand for memory chips, pushing phone makers to raise prices and abandon some of their most affordable models.
  FULL TEXT: The world’s cheapest smartphones are disappearing because AI infrastructure is absorbing the components. Prices of existing smartphone models have risen by about 15% globally this year , while newly launched models are roughly 25% more expensive than last year. A global memory chip shortage, driven by demand from AI data centers, is pushing up the cost of making smartphones. Chinese smartphone makers — which have championed affordable, feature-rich devices and account for approximately 60% of all smartphones shipped globally — have “drastically reduced” their entry-level projects this year as memory-chip costs have risen, Ivan Lam, senior analyst at research firm Counterpoint, told Rest of World . They are instead prioritizing more profitable premium devices. The smartphone price increases vary sharply by region: They have risen by 21% in India, 19% across Asia-Pacific, and 18% in the Middle East and Africa. In the U.S., they have gone up by just 5%. The shift could affect more than consumers looking for cheaper phones. For people who have yet to buy their first smartphone, higher prices could make it harder to get online at all — potentially widening the digital divide. The consequences will also reach the U.S. as more essential services move online and the global supply chain for smartphones becomes more expensive. GSMA, the global mobile industry association, had earlier said nearly 800 million more people will use mobile internet by 2030. But it is concerned that rising smartphone costs would affect this projection and widen the gap of digital inequality, Claire Sibthorpe, head of digital inclusion at the group’s nonprofit foundation, told Rest of World . “We live in an increasingly digital world, and the proliferation of technologies such as AI is creating greater digital divides and inequities,” Sibthorpe said. “Stakeholders are increasingly using AI to provide access to important services, but people won’t be able to benefit without access to an internet-enabled phone and the internet.” In 2025, more than one in four smartphones shipped globally cost less than $150 . That market is now shrinking. Chinese phonemakers including Xiaomi i Xiaomi Xiaomi is a Chinese consumer electronics company that leads the country in smartphone manufacturing and sales. * A life in episodes
  Aeon | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  For a decade, I’ve posted episodes of my memoir to Facebook. My readers correct my understanding of my past - by Thomas Söderqvist Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: When I was about 15, alone in the flat one afternoon, I sneaked into my mother’s writing desk and found a small pocket calendar from 1946 pushed to the back of a drawer. I had grown up with my mother and her parents, not knowing who my biological father was, or how I had come about; the question was simply never asked. I turned, with a kind of dread, to the entries from nine months before I was born. In mid-March there was an ordinary note – ‘Party at the Academic Association’; she was training as a laboratory technician in Lund and liked the students’ company – and on the following day, in her hand, five words: ‘God, what have I done!’ I still remember the shock of standing there with the diary, half-guilty for trespassing on her private papers, and saying aloud, over and over: ‘That’s me – that’s me. What have you done? I’m a mistake.’ For half a century, the scene stayed as it was: fixed, private, finished. Then, about a decade ago, I posted it – to several hundred old acquaintances and strangers – on Facebook. I was in doubt; I felt faint embarrassment at making it public, but this was itself part of what I wanted to examine. Why was it shameful? What did the shame tell me about myself? And would saying it out loud, to strangers, do anything to drain the memory of its sting? The answers came from my readers, and one of them showed me something I could not have seen on my own. Betty, a friend from the years we were postdocs together at Stanford, had heard me tell the story in person long ago, and she said the written version was missing something: my voice, the intonation and the look on my face. What look, I asked – had I played it for sympathy, had I ironised it? ‘Absolutely flat,’ she wrote. ‘No affect. A Max von Sydow deadpan. But yes, you ironised.’ I had not known that about myself: that I’d delivered the central wound of my origins with no expression at all and turned it into a dry joke. T hat discovery, prompted by a reader’s response, was one of the first signs that writing one’s life in public could change how memory and narrative take shape. Writing an autobiography or memoir was traditionally a solitary undertaking. Authors worked through papers, memories and past events largely on their own, occasionally consulting family, friends or colleagues. Only after years of private work would the manuscript reach the public. Readers encountered the finished account, with little sense of the revisions, doubts and shifts in understanding that had shap
  https://aeon.co/essays/what-i-have-learned-from-posting-episodes-of-my-life-on-facebook

* Sliman Mansour’s Art Captured the Palestinian Condition
  New Lines | 02 Oct 10:14 UTC
  Sliman Mansour spent a lifetime using his art to preserve Palestinian identity and connect people to their land, inspiring generations of artists in his wake. A writer who knew him from childhood as a close friend of her father remembers the gentle, humble man behind the iconic paintings. The post Sliman Mansour’s Art Captured the Palesti...
  FULL TEXT: When we speak of the Palestinian artist Sliman Mansour, the conversation too often narrows to a single painting, his “Camel of Hardships” (1973). It was through this painting that Mansour became known in Palestine and the Arab world. “Camel of Hardships” was inspired by the porters he saw on the streets of Jerusalem, when he passed through the Old City. It depicts an old man with traditional Palestinian clothes carrying the city of Jerusalem, as a heavy load, on his shoulder. The painting’s influence exploded with the decision to reproduce it as a poster, distributing and selling it at affordable prices for people to purchase and display in their homes. Seemingly every Palestinian home displayed “Camel of Hardships” on its walls. But while many were familiar with the poster, far fewer knew the artist who created it. That iconic painting has also dominated discussions since Mansour’s death on Aug. 24, at the age of 79, overshadowing the breadth of a career spanning more than five decades. Perhaps this is what made Mansour dislike that particular painting for such a long time. For him, although it was iconic and marked a phase in which he became more aware of his identity as a Palestinian artist, it nevertheless overshadowed much of his other work, work that people did not know much about until recent years, when he became active on social media. I knew Mansour as my father’s lifelong friend from my childhood onward. He was a gentle, humble man who frequented our home as if he were a member of the family. He was courteous, with a blond beard, a commanding presence and a cheerful soul. I remember him as a regular attendee of the League of Palestinian Artists meetings held on the long glass balcony at our home on Nablus Street in al-Bireh. I remember him sitting with my father for hours in our garden beneath the walnut tree on summer evenings, discussing art, exhibitions, artists’ meetings and elections. As a child, I would overhear the heated debates that punctuated these gatherings, along with the laughter and conversations fueled by boundless passion. The meetings often left behind clouds of white smoke tinged with the sweet aroma of pipe tobacco some artists favored, and ashtrays filled with ash and cigarette butts. Mansour and Nabil Anani met in the early 1970s, a few years after the 1967 war and occupation of all of historic Palestine. Along with other artists, they decided to form a body that would unite Palestinian artists and give them space to meet,

* Who gets to decide if AI is safe? Africa wants a say
  Rest of World | 02 Oct 10:00 UTC
  African companies race to adopt American and Chinese AI, with little attention paid to safety.
  FULL TEXT: African leaders called last week for a greater role in setting global standards for artificial intelligence. Their statements at the United Nations came amid warnings from Silicon Valley executives about AI models being misused , and sometimes going rogue and taking unauthorized actions. OpenAI’s agents hacked government websites across the world in June. Models from Anthropic , Google , and Meta , too, hacked into other companies’ systems during tests. “Africa … must be equal co-architects in determining the standards, ethics, and architectures of this technological era,” Lewis Garseedah Brown II, permanent representative of Liberia, said at a U.N. Security Council meeting on AI. African companies and governments are racing to deploy AI systems built in the U.S. and China. But many nations lack the ability to independently test whether those systems are safe for their populations, experts told Rest of World. “ There’s essentially no real discussion about AI safety. So, there’s a real vacuum,” said Jonathan Shock, associate professor in math at the University of Cape Town. If countries set up their own safeguards, it could complicate operations for American and Chinese companies in new markets. Fragmented regulations could make global deployments of models more costly and difficult. Incidents of AI misuse and malfunction are surfacing across Africa. Kenya’s predictive machine learning algorithm for setting health insurance contributions has driven up costs for poor households. Scammers in West Africa have used AI-generated content to blackmail victims in extortion schemes . The militant group Boko Haram has also used AI to organize attacks and design weapons after bypassing safeguards. “I think it’s going to take a potentially catastrophic incident for governments to really take [heed],” Shock said, which he dubbed his optimistic view. “The pessimistic view is we don’t stop it in time.” Africa currently lags behind other regions in AI adoption, though its companies are moving quickly. Out of 85 large companies surveyed by PricewaterhouseCoopers this year, more than 80% were running AI pilots. Many lack formal structures to oversee safety, according to the report. “Everyone is just trying to catch up [on AI deployment],” Mélanie Keïta, CEO and founder of Nairobi-based financing company Melanin Kapital, told Rest of World . Like many African companies, Melanin Kapital has weighed the choice between American and Chinese AI models, testing both internally. Bot
  https://restofworld.org/2026/african-leaders-un-security-council-ai-safety/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* Life on a hair trigger
  Aeon | 02 Oct 10:00 UTC
  A world of violence sustains itself through guns, poverty and other social ills, but also through the minds it creates - by Megan Kang Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Chicago, Illinois, US, 25 December 2021. Photo by Cheney Orr/Reuters The day before we spoke, King 1 , 26, had learned that his friend Lamont had been killed. He was at home when his cousin called to break the news and asked him to come over. ‘I’m on my way,’ King replied immediately, knowing what was being asked of him. He ran up to his room to retrieve his Glock, opened his box of bullets and, one by one, filled the clip. Lamont was dead, though King couldn’t fully wrap his mind around the fact. He’d seen him only hours earlier, when Lamont and King’s cousin stopped by his house. They’d been shot at by their rivals the day before, and King had warned them to be careful: ‘Y’all need to keep y’alls’ heads up.’ Now the car Lamont died in sat empty on a nearby street, shot up and stained with his blood. King watched a video posted on Facebook of paramedics draping the body with a sheet and carrying it into an ambulance. King knew what came next. He and his cousin would go out with their guns and ride through the neighbourhood until they found the men responsible. ‘I just wanted my get-back,’ he said. He wanted the people who killed Lamont to feel what he was feeling. And if he couldn’t find them, he’d find someone else he had beef with. King reasoned: ‘It could be a person who I don’t like or who I’m already into it with. I’m going to slide on them too and whoever else. That’s how I grew up with my feelings. That’s how I let off my rage.’ To King, retaliation felt less like a choice than an inevitability. ‘Whether I do it or not, somebody going to do it,’ he said. ‘If it ain’t me, I got other homies who feel the same way I feel. I got cousins, family members, they mommas. That’s how it be, and that’s how it’s going to always be. It’s a repeated cycle.’ His clip was full. Then King decided to do something that a year earlier he would not have done. F or King, and for many Americans like him, gun violence is not an exceptional event but a fact of life. In some parts of the United States, lethal violence has reached a scale exceeding recent wars and military occupations. Between 2020 and 2021, a young man in Chicago’s Garfield Park neighbourhood, where King is from, faced a higher risk of being killed than a US soldier deployed to Afghanistan or Iraq. By age 40, around half of the city’s Black and Hispanic residents will have witnessed someone being shot. Violent crime has fallen steeply across the US since 2021, though not everywhere.
